<a id="pm-s1"></a>
## 1. Abstract and result contract

> We prove the classification conjecture for connected smooth closed Einstein four-manifolds with strictly positive sectional curvature. Up to positive scaling and isometry, every such manifold is the round four-sphere, the complex projective plane with its Fubini–Study metric, or real projective four-space with its round metric. No orientability assumption is needed.

- **claim-classification** (classification, [source](#stmt-thm-classification)): Let $(M,g)$ be a connected smooth closed Einstein four-manifold with strictly positive sectional curvature, without an orientability assumption. There is a constant $a>0$ such that $(M,ag)$ is isometric to the standard round $S^4$, to $\mathbb{CP}^2$ with its Fubini–Study metric, or to $\mathbb{RP}^4$ with its standard round metric.
  - Explanation: Every connected smooth closed Einstein four-manifold with strictly positive sectional curvature is, after a positive rescaling, isometric to the round sphere, the complex projective plane with its Fubini–Study metric, or real projective four-space with its round metric. No orientability assumption is made.
- **claim-curvature-blocks** (theorem, [source](#stmt-lem-curvature-blocks)): There are symmetric trace-free endomorphisms $T\in\operatorname{Sym}_0(\Lambda^+)$ and $U\in\operatorname{Sym}_0(\Lambda^-)$ such that the curvature operator is $$\mathcal R_{\Lambda^2} =\begin{pmatrix}\operatorname{Id}+T&0\\ 0&\operatorname{Id}+U\end{pmatrix}.$$ At a point where these endomorphisms are diagonal, with eigenvalues $t_s,u_s$, the curvature tensor is $$R_{ikjl} =\frac12\sum_s(1+t_s)I_{s,ik}I_{s,jl} +\frac12\sum_s(1+u_s)J_{s,ik}J_{s,jl}.$$ Put $$x=-\min_s t_s,\qquad y=-\min_s u_s,\qquad v=\frac{\|T\|}{\sqrt6},\qquad b=\frac{\|U\|}{\sqrt6},$$ where the matrix norms are Hilbert–Schmidt norms. Then $$\sec_g>0\quad\Longleftrightarrow\quad x+y<2, \qquad v\le x\le2v,\qquad b\le y\le2b.$$ Both Weyl blocks, viewed as curvature four-tensors, have zero divergence. In particular, for $Z=\nabla T$, whose matrix entries are one-forms, the differential Bianchi constraint is $$\sum_s I_s Z_{sr}=0\qquad(r=1,2,3).$$
  - Explanation: Under the Einstein condition the curvature operator is block diagonal on self-dual and anti-self-dual two-forms, with trace-free Weyl blocks. Sectional positivity is equivalent to a condition on the smallest eigenvalues, which are related to the block norms. The Bianchi identity gives a divergence-free constraint on the derivative of a block.
- **claim-spin-algebra** (theorem, [source](#stmt-lem-spin-algebra)): A spin-$j$ representation has weights $-j,-j+1,\ldots,j$ for $\Sigma_3/(2i)$, each once, and $$H_j\otimes H_\ell \cong H_{j+\ell}\oplus H_{j+\ell-1}\oplus\cdots\oplus H_{\|j-\ell\|}.$$ In particular, the tangent, plus two-form, and plus trace-free symmetric-matrix representations have types $$\left(\frac12,\frac12\right),\qquad (1,0),\qquad (2,0).$$ The Casimir operators and the orthogonal projections onto these irreducible types are parallel bundle endomorphisms.
  - Explanation: Spin representations have a prescribed weight list and a tensor product rule with multiplicity one. The tangent, plus two-form and trace-free symmetric types are identified, and the type projections are parallel.
- **claim-quadratic-curvature-action** (bound, [source](#stmt-lem-quadratic-curvature-action)): If $A$ has smallest eigenvalue $-x$, then $$Q_j(A)\le \frac{x(j+1)}{2j-1}\operatorname{Id}.$$ Moreover, $Q_2(A)=B_F(A)$. The compression of $B_F(A)$, acting on an $F$ factor, to a highest-spin coupling of total plus spin $j$ is $Q_j(A)$, provided the operator acts trivially on any additional irreducible minus factor.
  - Explanation: A curvature-weighted quadratic operator on a spin representation is bounded above in terms of the smallest eigenvalue of the weighting matrix. In the spin-two case it equals the explicit matrix-slot operator. Its compression to a stretched coupling is the same operator.
- **claim-weyl-derivative-equations** (theorem, [source](#stmt-prop-weyl-derivative-equations)): The tensor $Z=\nabla T$ belongs to the parallel subbundle $G$ of type $(5/2,1/2)$. On this bundle, put $B_{\mathrm{op}}=Q_{5/2}(T)$. Then $$\Delta T=6(T-N),\qquad N=(T^2)_0=T^2-2v^2\operatorname{Id},$$ and $$\Delta Z=(13-20B_{\mathrm{op}})Z. \tag{1}\label{eq:1}$$ Every statement has an exchanged counterpart for $U$.
  - Explanation: The derivative of a Weyl block lies in a single parallel irreducible subbundle. The block satisfies an explicit Laplace equation, and its derivative satisfies a Laplace equation involving a curvature-weighted operator.
- **claim-gradient-parametrization** (theorem, [source](#stmt-lem-gradient-parametrization)): There are unique vectors $k,l$ such that $$\begin{gathered} Z_{ii}=s_i p-\sqrt3\,\widetilde s_i k,\qquad Z_{jk}=-I_i\left(l+s_i k+\frac{\widetilde s_i}{\sqrt3}p\right), \qquad (i,j,k)\ \text{cyclic},\\ e=\frac53\|p\|^2+5\|k\|^2+\|l\|^2. \end{gathered} \tag{2}\label{eq:2}$$ More generally, for any fixed normalized trace-free diagonal triple $s$, the same formulas parametrize the entire algebraic space $G$ by three arbitrary real four-vectors.
  - Explanation: The Bianchi-constrained derivative space is parametrized by three vectors, one of which is the gradient of the block norm. The energy splits as an explicit sum of squares, which yields the refined Kato inequality.
- **claim-weak-norm-equation** (theorem, [source](#stmt-prop-weak-norm-equation)): The function $e/v$, defined to be zero on $\{v=0\}$, belongs to $L^1(M)$, and $$\Delta v=6v(1-\sigma v)+\frac{e-\|p\|^2}{v} \tag{3}\label{eq:3}$$ holds as an equality of distributions with an $L^1$ density. The quotient is zero almost everywhere on the zero set. This identity may be tested against bounded Lipschitz functions. The corresponding assertions hold for $b$.
  - Explanation: The norm of a Weyl block satisfies its Laplace equation as a distributional identity with an integrable density, with no singular measure on the zero set.
- **claim-weighted-variance** (bound, [source](#stmt-prop-weighted-variance)): For every real $a\in H^1(M)$, $$\operatorname{Var}(a) :=\mathbf E\bigl[(a-\mathbf E a)^2\bigr] \le\mathbf E\frac{\|\nabla a\|^2}{K}, \qquad K=4+v^2+b^2. \tag{4}\label{eq:4}$$
  - Explanation: The variance of any Sobolev function is bounded by the mean of its squared gradient divided by a curvature-dependent positive weight, which strengthens the Poincaré inequality.
- **claim-sharp-sobolev** (bound, [source](#stmt-prop-sharp-sobolev)): For every real $f\in H^1(M)$, $$\bigl(\mathbf E\|f\|^4\bigr)^{1/2} \le\mathbf E\left(f^2+\frac12\|\nabla f\|^2\right).$$
  - Explanation: A sharp normalized Sobolev inequality controls the fourth-moment norm of a Sobolev function by its squared norm and gradient energy.
- **claim-weyl-gap** (bound, [source](#stmt-prop-weyl-gap)): For either Weyl block, $$v\equiv0\qquad\text{or}\qquad \mathbf E v^2\ge1. \tag{5}\label{eq:5}$$ If $T\not\equiv0$ and $\mathbf E v^2=1$, then $\nabla T=0$ and $v=\sigma=1$. The corresponding statements hold for $U$.
  - Explanation: A Weyl block either vanishes identically or has second moment at least one. In the equality case its derivative vanishes and its norm and shape invariant are one.
- **claim-characteristic-identities** (theorem, [source](#stmt-prop-characteristic-identities)): Let $n_+$ and $n_-$ be the finite nonnegative integer dimensions of the spaces of harmonic self-dual and anti-self-dual two-forms. The Euler characteristic and signature of $M$ are, respectively, $$\chi=2+n_++n_-,\qquad \tau=n_+-n_-.$$ Then $$\begin{aligned} 8\pi^2\chi &=6\operatorname{Vol}(M) \bigl(1+\mathbf E(v^2+b^2)\bigr),\\ 12\pi^2\tau &=6\operatorname{Vol}(M)\,\mathbf E(v^2-b^2). \end{aligned} \tag{6}\label{eq:6}$$ If $U=0$, then $n_-=0$; likewise $T=0$ implies $n_+=0$.
  - Explanation: The Euler characteristic and signature are expressed through the harmonic form dimensions and through volume and second moments of the two block norms. A vanishing block forces the corresponding harmonic space to be trivial.
- **claim-upper-volume-deficit** (bound, [source](#stmt-prop-upper-volume-deficit)): One has $$\frac{V_M}{8\pi^2/3}\le 1-\frac{3}{40}s_0. \tag{7}\label{eq:7}$$
  - Explanation: The volume is bounded above by the round value times a deficit that is linear in the total second moment of the two block norms.
- **claim-geodesic-averages** (theorem, [source](#stmt-lem-geodesic-averages)): At every time along the geodesic flow, $$\mathcal E\|A\|^2=\frac32s_0,\qquad \mathcal E\|A'\|^2\le\frac32\,\mathcal E\|A\|^2. \tag{8}\label{eq:8}$$
  - Explanation: Averages over the unit tangent bundle of the squared curvature deviation matrix and of its derivative along geodesics are expressed through the block moments.
- **claim-volume-topology** (theorem, [source](#stmt-lem-volume-topology)): The diameter of $M$ is at most $\pi$, its injectivity radius is at least $\pi/\sqrt3$, and $M$ is simply connected. Every minimizing geodesic segment has no point conjugate to its initial point in its interior.
  - Explanation: The diameter is bounded above, the injectivity radius is bounded below, and the manifold is simply connected. Minimizing geodesic segments have no interior conjugate point.
- **claim-upper-polar** (bound, [source](#stmt-lem-upper-polar)): One has $$V_M\le2\pi^2\int_0^\pi\mathcal E D_c(t)\,dt.$$
  - Explanation: The volume is bounded above by a polar-coordinate integral of the averaged truncated Jacobi determinant.
- **claim-discrete-jacobi** (theorem, [source](#stmt-lem-discrete-jacobi)): Fix a smooth symmetric normal curvature matrix on $[0,t]$, put $a=t/N$, and let $\mathcal L_N$ be the block tridiagonal matrix on nodes $1,\ldots,N-1$ with diagonal blocks $2\mathrm{Id}-a^2\mathcal R(ia)$ and off-diagonal blocks $-\mathrm{Id}$. Then $$a^3\det\mathcal L_N\longrightarrow D(t).$$ If $t$ is before the first conjugate time, $\mathcal L_N$ is positive definite for all sufficiently large $N$. The Jacobi convergence is uniform over families with uniformly bounded coefficients and first derivatives.
  - Explanation: A block tridiagonal Dirichlet matrix approximates the Jacobi determinant, is positive definite before the first conjugate time for large size, and the convergence is uniform under bounded coefficients.
- **claim-upper-radial** (bound, [source](#stmt-lem-upper-radial)): For $0<t<\pi$, set $$\beta=\frac{t^2}{\pi^2-t^2},\qquad B=\frac2t\int_0^t A(s)\sin^2(\pi s/t)\,ds.$$ Before conjugacy, $$D(t)\le\sin^3t\,\det(\mathrm{Id}-\beta B), \qquad \mathrm{Id}-\beta B\succeq0.$$ At every time $0<t<\pi$, $$1-\frac{D_c(t)}{\sin^3t} \ge\frac16\min\{1,\beta^2\max(1,3-\beta)\}\,\|B\|^2.$$
  - Explanation: Before conjugacy the Jacobi determinant is bounded by a sine power times a determinant involving a sine-weighted average of the curvature deviation. The determinant loss is bounded below by the squared norm of that average.
- **claim-sine-minorant** (bound, [source](#stmt-lem-sine-minorant)): Put $$a_0=\frac{157}{50},\qquad b_0=\frac{22}{7},\qquad S(z)=a_0z-\frac{(b_0z)^3}{6} +\frac{(a_0z)^5}{120}-\frac{(b_0z)^7}{5040}.$$ The constants satisfy $a_0<\pi<b_0$, and $0\le S(z)\le\sin(\pi z)$ for $0\le z\le1/2$. Its coefficients are $$S(z)=\frac{157}{50}z-\frac{5324}{1029}z^3 +\frac{95388992557}{37500000000}z^5 -\frac{155897368}{259416045}z^7.$$
  - Explanation: A rational polynomial with explicit coefficients is a nonnegative lower bound for the sine on the half interval, built from rational bounds on pi.
- **claim-moment-obstruction** (theorem, [source](#stmt-lem-moment-obstruction)): Every probability measure $\mu$ on $[0,2]$ satisfying $$\int z^2\,d\mu\ge1,\qquad \int(28z+6z^2)\,d\mu\le25$$ satisfies $$16\left(\int H\,d\mu\right)^2-\int\Phi\,d\mu<0.$$
  - Explanation: No probability measure on the interval can satisfy the two moment constraints while making the stated functional nonnegative.
- **claim-balanced-moments** (theorem, [source](#stmt-prop-balanced-moments)): If neither Weyl block is identically zero, then $\tau=0$ and $\mathbf E v^2=\mathbf E b^2$.
  - Explanation: If both Weyl blocks are nonzero, the signature vanishes and the two second moments are equal.
- **claim-hessian-components** (theorem, [source](#stmt-lem-hessian-components)): Let $\mathcal H=T^*M\otimes G$, and write $X=\nabla Z$, so $X_{ij}=\nabla_i\nabla_jT$. Then $$X=Y+D_0, \qquad Y\text{ has type }(3,1),\qquad D_0\text{ has type }(2,0).$$ If $\operatorname{Tr}:\mathcal H\to F$ is contraction of the two derivative slots and $\operatorname{Tr}^*$ its adjoint, then $$\operatorname{Tr}\operatorname{Tr}^*=\frac{12}{5}\mathrm{Id}, \qquad D_0=\frac5{12}\operatorname{Tr}^*\Delta T, \qquad \|D_0\|^2=\frac5{12}\|\Delta T\|^2.$$ All the type projections in this statement are parallel.
  - Explanation: The Hessian of a Weyl block splits into a highest type and a trace type. The trace type is determined by the Laplacian, and all type projections are parallel.
- **claim-hessian-operator** (theorem, [source](#stmt-lem-hessian-operator)): The combined compressed operator $\mathcal P$ preserves all four types in $\mathcal H$. It is positive definite on $(3,1)$, and on the trace type it satisfies $$\mathcal P\operatorname{Tr}^* =\operatorname{Tr}^*(4-B_F(T)).$$ The individual derivative-slot and matrix-slot summands need not preserve the four types.
  - Explanation: The combined curvature-weighted operator preserves the four Hessian types, is positive definite on the highest type, and acts on the trace type by an explicit formula.
- **claim-weighted-hessian** (theorem, [source](#stmt-prop-weighted-hessian)): For every global Lipschitz scalar weight $m\ge m_0>0$, $$0\le\mathbf E\left[ mG_v(p,k,l) -\frac56\sum_i m_i\, \frac{\operatorname{tr}\bigl(Z_i\sum_jZ_j^2\bigr)}6 +\frac{\langle\mathcal P(L_m)_Y,(L_m)_Y\rangle}{24m} \right], \tag{10}\label{eq:10}$$ where $i,j$ in the middle term are derivative indices and $$G_v=\left(-42-3b^2-\frac{10}{3}v^2\right)e +75B+\frac{25}{3}C_N-\frac{100}{3}E_B +5v^2\|p\|^2.$$
  - Explanation: Completing a weighted square of the free Hessian component and integrating by parts gives an inequality involving only first derivatives of the block, valid for any positive Lipschitz weight.
- **claim-hessian-matrices** (construction, [source](#stmt-lem-hessian-matrices)): At a point where $v>0$, use the three vector parameters $p,k,l$ in <a href="#eq:2" data-reference-type="eqref" data-reference="eq:2">[eq:2]</a>. Then $G_v$ is the quadratic form, with scalar entries multiplying vector dot products, whose matrix is $$\begin{pmatrix} -70-5b^2+100\sigma v-25v^2 &-100\sqrt3\rho v&-35\rho v^2/\sqrt3\\ -100\sqrt3\rho v &-210-15b^2-300\sigma v-190v^2&-75v-35\sigma v^2\\ -35\rho v^2/\sqrt3 &-75v-35\sigma v^2&-42-3b^2-10v^2 \end{pmatrix}. \tag{11}\label{eq:11}$$
  - Explanation: In the three vector parameters the quadratic form of the weighted inequality is given by an explicit symmetric matrix of scalar entries.
- **claim-cubic-contraction** (bound, [source](#stmt-lem-cubic-contraction)): For every real tangent vector $a$, with $Z_a=\sum_i a_iZ_i$, $$\left\|\frac{\operatorname{tr}\bigl(Z_a\sum_jZ_j^2\bigr)}6\right\| \le \frac45\sqrt{\frac35}\,\|a\|e^{3/2}. \tag{13}\label{eq:13}$$
  - Explanation: The cubic contraction of the derivative of a block against a direction is bounded by the direction length times the energy to the three halves power.
- **claim-real-vector-projections** (theorem, [source](#stmt-lem-real-vector-projections)): Let $P_+$ be the projection in $\mathcal H$ onto total plus spin $3$, retaining both possible minus spins. For any real tangent vector $a$ and any $Z\in G$, $$\|P_+(a\otimes Z)\|^2=\frac7{12}\|a\|^2\|Z\|^2,$$ $$\left\langle\widetilde QP_+(a\otimes Z),P_+(a\otimes Z)\right\rangle =\frac12\|a\|^2\langle Q_{5/2}(T)Z,Z\rangle.$$
  - Explanation: Projection of a real vector tensored with a gradient-space element onto the highest plus spin has explicitly computed norm and curvature-weighted quadratic form.
- **claim-hessian-projection-cost** (bound, [source](#stmt-prop-hessian-projection-cost)): For the weight <a href="#eq:12" data-reference-type="eqref" data-reference="eq:12">[eq:12]</a>, $$\frac{\langle\mathcal P(L_m)_Y,(L_m)_Y\rangle}{24m} \le\frac9{64}\|p-q\|^2 \left[\frac7{12}(4+y)e-\frac54B\right]. \tag{14}\label{eq:14}$$ The bracket on the right is nonnegative. Both assertions remain valid under the closed inequalities $v+b\le2$ and $x+y\le2$.
  - Explanation: For the chosen weights the projected quadratic cost is bounded by a nonnegative expression in the gradient difference, the energy and a curvature-weighted term.
- **claim-coupling-estimates** (theorem, [source](#stmt-prop-coupling-estimates)): For this decomposition, define $$r=R_v\|p\|^2+R_b\|q\|^2,\qquad j=(\|p\|^2+\|q\|^2) \left(\frac{\|p\|^2}{v}+\frac{\|q\|^2}{b}\right).$$ Then, almost everywhere, $$\mathcal I_4\le-cj,\qquad \mathcal I_2\le2\Theta r,\qquad \mathcal I_0\le-\frac{\Theta^2}{c}.$$ The corresponding derivative summands are assigned value zero on a zero set, as in <a href="#eq:3" data-reference-type="eqref" data-reference="eq:3">[eq:3]</a>. The total integrand and the bounds are integrable, and $\mathbf E\mathcal I\ge0$.
  - Explanation: The combined integrand splits by derivative degree into quartic, quadratic and potential parts, each bounded pointwise, and has nonnegative mean.
- **claim-bernstein-conversion** (construction, [source](#stmt-lem-bernstein-conversion)): Let $p$ be a polynomial of degree at most $N$ on an interval $[r,s]$, and write $$p(r+(s-r)z)=\sum_{j=0}^N a_jz^j.$$ Its degree-$N$ Bernstein expansion is $$p(r+(s-r)z)=\sum_{i=0}^N h_i\binom Ni z^i(1-z)^{N-i}, \qquad h_i=\sum_{j=0}^i a_j\frac{\binom ij}{\binom Nj}.$$ In particular, $h_i\ge\eta$ for all $i$ implies $p\ge\eta$ on $[r,s]$.* *For a polynomial on a triangle with ordered vertices $p_\ell=(r_\ell,s_\ell)$, set $$V=r_3+(r_1-r_3)z+(r_2-r_3)w,\qquad W=s_3+(s_1-s_3)z+(s_2-s_3)w.$$ If $P(z,w)=p(V,W)=\sum_{d,e}P_{de}z^dw^e$ has degree at most $N$, then its normalized degree-$N$ triangular coefficients are $$[p]_{ij}=s_{ij}(P):= \sum_{d=0}^i\sum_{e=0}^j \frac{\binom id\binom je} {\binom N{d+e}\binom{d+e}d}\,P_{de}, \qquad i,j\ge0,\quad i+j\le N.$$ On $z,w\ge0$, $z+w\le1$, the polynomial is the sum of these coefficients times the nonnegative basis functions $$\frac{N!}{i!j!(N-i-j)!} z^iw^j(1-z-w)^{N-i-j}.$$ These basis functions sum to one. The same expansion applies entrywise to a symmetric polynomial matrix: positive semidefiniteness of every coefficient matrix implies positive semidefiniteness throughout the triangle.
  - Explanation: Explicit formulas convert polynomial coefficients to Bernstein coefficients on intervals and triangles, so positivity of the coefficients certifies positivity, including for matrix-valued polynomials.
- **claim-scalar-certificates** (theorem, [source](#stmt-lem-scalar-certificates)): For the polynomials in Section <a href="#sec:coupling" data-reference-type="ref" data-reference="sec:coupling">6</a>, the following bounds hold: $$\begin{gathered} C_0>0,\qquad 4C_0\left((h-A_0)(2-v)+\frac25hv\right) -(2-v)(2A_0+L_0)^2>0\quad(0\le v\le2),\\ G,H>0\quad(0\le u\le1),\qquad Z_*\le0\quad(0\le u\le1/16),\\ Z_*^2\le64A^2u^2GH\quad(1/16\le u\le1). \end{gathered}$$
  - Explanation: The scalar polynomial inequalities used in the quartic estimate hold on their stated intervals, certified by Bernstein coefficient bounds.
- **claim-matrix-certificates** (theorem, [source](#stmt-lem-matrix-certificates)): The symmetric matrix represented by $\mathcal A$ is positive semidefinite on $$\mathcal D=\{(v,b):v,b\ge0,\ v+b\le2\}.$$ The symmetric matrix represented by $\mathcal B$ is positive semidefinite on $$\mathcal D_+=\{(v,b)\in\mathcal D:v\ge b\}.$$ On $\mathcal D_+$ one also has $$L_v\ge0,\qquad F+12c\delta^2L_b\ge0.$$
  - Explanation: Two symmetric polynomial matrices are positive semidefinite on the spectral domain and its ordered half, and two further scalar polynomials are nonnegative there, certified on triangular subdivisions.
- **claim-potential-certificate** (theorem, [source](#stmt-lem-potential-certificate)): The polynomial $F=cE_0-\Theta^2$ is nonnegative on $\mathcal D$.
  - Explanation: The potential polynomial is nonnegative on the full spectral domain, proved by a change of coordinates and Bernstein coefficients.
- **claim-one-block-vanishes** (theorem, [source](#stmt-prop-one-block-vanishes)): At least one of $T,U$ vanishes identically; equivalently, the metric is half-conformally flat.
  - Explanation: At least one Weyl block vanishes identically, so the metric is half-conformally flat. The proof combines the coupled integral estimate with strictness of a Cauchy–Schwarz step and the two block gaps.
- **claim-one-block-alternatives** (theorem, [source](#stmt-lem-one-block-alternatives)): Suppose $U=0$ and $T\not\equiv0$. Then $$\frac{V_M}{\pi^2}=\frac{8-2n_+}{3},\qquad s_0=\mathbf E v^2=\frac{3n_+}{4-n_+},\qquad n_+=1\text{ or }2.$$ In the second case, $s_0=3$ and $V_M=4\pi^2/3$.
  - Explanation: With one block zero and the other nonzero, volume and second moment are given in terms of the harmonic count, which has only two possible values.
- **claim-conditional-lower-volume** (conditional_result, [source](#stmt-prop-conditional-lower-volume)): If $s_0=\mathbf E(v^2+b^2)=3$, then $V_M>4\pi^2/3$.
  - Explanation: Under the exceptional total moment value the volume strictly exceeds the exceptional volume, which excludes the second harmonic alternative.
- **claim-lower-polar** (bound, [source](#stmt-lem-lower-polar)): One has $$V_M\ge2\pi^2\int_0^{\pi/\sqrt3}\mathcal E D(t)\,dt.$$
  - Explanation: The volume is bounded below by a polar integral of the averaged Jacobi determinant up to the injectivity radius bound.
- **claim-lower-radial** (bound, [source](#stmt-lem-lower-radial)): If $s_0=3$ and $0<t<\pi/\sqrt3$, then, with $\theta=\sqrt3t$, $$\mathcal E D(t)\ge t^3\left(\frac{\sin\theta}{\theta}\right)^{5/6} e^{-\theta^2/36}.$$ Moreover, $$-\log\left(\frac{\sin\theta}{\theta}\right)-\frac{\theta^2}{6} \ge\frac12\left(\frac\theta\pi\right)^4 +\frac13\left(\frac\theta\pi\right)^6.$$
  - Explanation: Under the exceptional moment the averaged Jacobi determinant is bounded below by an explicit expression, and a logarithmic sine inequality holds.
- **claim-parallel-models** (classification, [source](#stmt-prop-parallel-models)): Up to reversing orientation, either $T=U=0$, or $U=0$ and $T$ is parallel with eigenvalues $2,-1,-1$.
  - Explanation: After possibly reversing orientation, either both blocks vanish or one block vanishes and the other is parallel with the stated eigenvalues.
- **claim-model-curvature** (theorem, [source](#stmt-lem-model-curvature)): The two tensors in Proposition <a href="#prop:parallel-models" data-reference-type="ref" data-reference="prop:parallel-models">38</a> are the curvature tensors of the unit round sphere and of twice the Fubini–Study metric induced by the Hopf quotient of the unit sphere $S^5\subset\mathbb C^3$.
  - Explanation: The two remaining curvature tensors are those of the unit round sphere and of a rescaled Fubini–Study metric obtained from the Hopf quotient.
- **claim-global-isometry** (theorem, [source](#stmt-lem-global-isometry)): The local identifications furnished by Lemma <a href="#lem:model-curvature" data-reference-type="ref" data-reference="lem:model-curvature">39</a> extend to global isometries.
  - Explanation: Local identifications with the model spaces extend to global isometries, using parallel curvature and simple connectivity.
- **claim-oriented-classification** (classification, [source](#stmt-cor-oriented-classification)): Every connected oriented smooth closed Einstein four-manifold with strictly positive sectional curvature is, after positive scaling, isometric to the round $S^4$ or to $\mathbb{CP}^2$ with its Fubini–Study metric.
  - Explanation: Every connected oriented closed Einstein four-manifold with strictly positive sectional curvature is, after scaling, the round sphere or the complex projective plane with its Fubini–Study metric.
- **claim-form-complex-laplacians** (theorem, [source](#stmt-lem-form-complex-laplacians)): Consider the elliptic complex $$\Lambda^0\xrightarrow{\ d\ }\Lambda^1 \xrightarrow{\ \sqrt2\,d^+\ }\Lambda^+, \qquad d^+=P_+d.$$ Its three Laplacians are the Hodge Laplacians on the indicated bundles. Written as $-\Delta+E$, their potentials are $$E_0=0,\qquad E_1=3\operatorname{Id},\qquad E_+=4\operatorname{Id}-2T.$$ Its alternating heat trace is $1+n_+$.
  - Explanation: The three-term form complex has Hodge Laplacians with explicit zeroth-order potentials, and its alternating heat trace equals one plus the self-dual harmonic count.
- **claim-signed-heat-coefficient** (theorem, [source](#stmt-lem-signed-heat-coefficient)): For the three operators in Lemma <a href="#lem:form-complex-laplacians" data-reference-type="ref" data-reference="lem:form-complex-laplacians">42</a>, let $\operatorname{str}$ denote the trace on $\Lambda^0$ minus that on $\Lambda^1$ plus that on $\Lambda^+$. Write $\Omega$ for their connection curvatures. The constant local coefficient of their alternating heat trace, including the factor $(4\pi)^{-2}$, is $$\frac1{16\pi^2} \left(\frac12\operatorname{str}E^2 +\frac1{12}\operatorname{str}\sum_{k,i}\Omega_{ki}^2\right).$$
  - Explanation: The constant local coefficient of the alternating heat trace is given by supertraces of the squared potential and of the squared connection curvature.

      "exact_statement": "There are symmetric trace-free endomorphisms $T\\in\\operatorname{Sym}_0(\\Lambda^+)$ and $U\\in\\operatorname{Sym}_0(\\Lambda^-)$ such that the curvature operator is $$\\mathcal R_{\\Lambda^2}\n =\\begin{pmatrix}\\operatorname{Id}+T&0\\\\\n                  0&\\operatorname{Id}+U\\end{pmatrix}.$$ At a point where these endomorphisms are diagonal, with eigenvalues $t_s,u_s$, the curvature tensor is $$R_{ikjl}\n =\\frac12\\sum_s(1+t_s)I_{s,ik}I_{s,jl}\n  +\\frac12\\sum_s(1+u_s)J_{s,ik}J_{s,jl}.$$ Put $$x=-\\min_s t_s,\\qquad y=-\\min_s u_s,\\qquad\n v=\\frac{|T|}{\\sqrt6},\\qquad b=\\frac{|U|}{\\sqrt6},$$ where the matrix norms are Hilbert–Schmidt norms. Then $$\\sec_g>0\\quad\\Longleftrightarrow\\quad x+y<2,\n \\qquad\n v\\le x\\le2v,\\qquad b\\le y\\le2b.$$ Both Weyl blocks, viewed as curvature four-tensors, have zero divergence. In particular, for $Z=\\nabla T$, whose matrix entries are one-forms, the differential Bianchi constraint is $$\\sum_s I_s Z_{sr}=0\\qquad(r=1,2,3).$$",
      "explanation": "Under the Einstein condition the curvature operator is block diagonal on self-dual and anti-self-dual two-forms, with trace-free Weyl blocks. Sectional positivity is equivalent to a condition on the smallest eigenvalues, which are related to the block norms. The Bianchi identity gives a divergence-free constraint on the derivative of a block.",
      "fidelity": "PENDING",
      "hypotheses": [

          "anchor": "sec:preliminaries",

          "source_id": "PM-348.3"
        },
        {
          "anchor": "pm-h-8",
          "exact_quote": "On an oriented Euclidean four-space, choose triples of skew orthogonal complex structures $I_1,I_2,I_3$ and $J_1,J_2,J_3$ satisfying $$I_1I_2=I_3=-I_2I_1,\\qquad\n J_1J_2=J_3=-J_2J_1,$$ and their cyclic analogues, with every $I_s$ commuting with every $J_t$.",
          "source_id": "PM-348.3"
        },

    {
      "conclusion": {
        "anchor": "stmt-lem-spin-algebra",
        "exact_quote": "A spin-$j$ representation has weights $-j,-j+1,\\ldots,j$ for $\\Sigma_3/(2i)$, each once, and $$H_j\\otimes H_\\ell\n \\cong H_{j+\\ell}\\oplus H_{j+\\ell-1}\\oplus\\cdots\\oplus H_{|j-\\ell|}.$$ In particular, the tangent, plus two-form, and plus trace-free symmetric-matrix representations have types $$\\left(\\frac12,\\frac12\\right),\\qquad (1,0),\\qquad (2,0).$$ The Casimir operators and the orthogonal projections onto these irreducible types are parallel bundle endomorphisms.",

      },

    {
      "conclusion": {
        "anchor": "stmt-lem-gradient-parametrization",
        "exact_quote": "There are unique vectors $k,l$ such that $$\\begin{gathered}\n Z_{ii}=s_i p-\\sqrt3\\,\\widetilde s_i k,\\qquad\n Z_{jk}=-I_i\\left(l+s_i k+\\frac{\\widetilde s_i}{\\sqrt3}p\\right),\n \\qquad (i,j,k)\\ \\text{cyclic},\\\\\n e=\\frac53|p|^2+5|k|^2+|l|^2.\n \\end{gathered}\n \\tag{2}\\label{eq:2}$$ More generally, for any fixed normalized trace-free diagonal triple $s$, the same formulas parametrize the entire algebraic space $G$ by three arbitrary real four-vectors.",
        "source_id": "PM-348.3"
      },
      "evidence": [

          "anchor": "stmt-lem-gradient-parametrization",

          "source_id": "PM-348.3"

      "exact_statement": "There are unique vectors $k,l$ such that $$\\begin{gathered}\n Z_{ii}=s_i p-\\sqrt3\\,\\widetilde s_i k,\\qquad\n Z_{jk}=-I_i\\left(l+s_i k+\\frac{\\widetilde s_i}{\\sqrt3}p\\right),\n \\qquad (i,j,k)\\ \\text{cyclic},\\\\\n e=\\frac53|p|^2+5|k|^2+|l|^2.\n \\end{gathered}\n \\tag{2}\\label{eq:2}$$ More generally, for any fixed normalized trace-free diagonal triple $s$, the same formulas parametrize the entire algebraic space $G$ by three arbitrary real four-vectors.",

    {
      "conclusion": {
        "anchor": "stmt-prop-characteristic-identities",
        "exact_quote": "Let $n_+$ and $n_-$ be the finite nonnegative integer dimensions of the spaces of harmonic self-dual and anti-self-dual two-forms. The Euler characteristic and signature of $M$ are, respectively, $$\\chi=2+n_++n_-,\\qquad \\tau=n_+-n_-.$$ Then $$\\begin{aligned}\n 8\\pi^2\\chi\n   &=6\\operatorname{Vol}(M)\n        \\bigl(1+\\mathbf E(v^2+b^2)\\bigr),\\\\\n 12\\pi^2\\tau\n   &=6\\operatorname{Vol}(M)\\,\\mathbf E(v^2-b^2).\n \\end{aligned}\n \\tag{6}\\label{eq:6}$$ If $U=0$, then $n_-=0$; likewise $T=0$ implies $n_+=0$.",
        "source_id": "PM-348.3"
      },
      "evidence": [
        {
          "anchor": "stmt-prop-characteristic-identities",
          "exact_quote": "Let $n_+$ and $n_-$ be the finite nonnegative integer dimensions of the spaces of harmonic self-dual and anti-self-dual two-forms.           "source_id": "PM-348.3"
        }
      ],
      "exact_statement": "Let $n_+$ and $n_-$ be the finite nonnegative integer dimensions of the spaces of harmonic self-dual and anti-self-dual two-forms.         "anchor": "stmt-lem-hessian-components",
        "exact_quote": "Let $\\mathcal H=T^*M\\otimes G$, and write $X=\\nabla Z$, so $X_{ij}=\\nabla_i\\nabla_jT$.       },

      "exact_statement": "For every global Lipschitz scalar weight $m\\ge m_0>0$, $$0\\le\\mathbf E\\left[\n mG_v(p,k,l)\n -\\frac56\\sum_i m_i\\,\n    \\frac{\\operatorname{tr}\\bigl(Z_i\\sum_jZ_j^2\\bigr)}6\n +\\frac{\\langle\\mathcal P(L_m)_Y,(L_m)_Y\\rangle}{24m}\n \\right],\n \\tag{10}\\label{eq:10}$$ where $i,j$ in the middle term are derivative indices and $$G_v=\\left(-42-3b^2-\\frac{10}{3}v^2\\right)e\n       +75B+\\frac{25}{3}C_N-\\frac{100}{3}E_B\n       +5v^2|p|^2.$$",

        "exact_quote": "For this decomposition, define $$r=R_v|p|^2+R_b|q|^2,\\qquad\n j=(|p|^2+|q|^2)\n       \\left(\\frac{|p|^2}{v}+\\frac{|q|^2}{b}\\right).$$ Then, almost everywhere, $$\\mathcal I_4\\le-cj,\\qquad\n \\mathcal I_2\\le2\\Theta r,\\qquad\n \\mathcal I_0\\le-\\frac{\\Theta^2}{c}.$$ The corresponding derivative summands are assigned value zero on a zero set, as in <a href=\"#eq:3\" data-reference-type=\"eqref\" data-reference=\"eq:3\">[eq:3]</a>. The total integrand and the bounds are integrable, and $\\mathbf E\\mathcal I\\ge0$.",

      },

      "conclusion": {
        "anchor": "stmt-lem-bernstein-conversion",
        "exact_quote": "Let $p$ be a polynomial of degree at most $N$ on an interval $[r,s]$, and write $$p(r+(s-r)z)=\\sum_{j=0}^N a_jz^j.$$ Its degree-$N$ Bernstein expansion is $$p(r+(s-r)z)=\\sum_{i=0}^N h_i\\binom Ni z^i(1-z)^{N-i},\n \\qquad\n h_i=\\sum_{j=0}^i a_j\\frac{\\binom ij}{\\binom Nj}.$$ In particular, $h_i\\ge\\eta$ for all $i$ implies $p\\ge\\eta$ on $[r,s]$.*\n\n*For a polynomial on a triangle with ordered vertices $p_\\ell=(r_\\ell,s_\\ell)$, set $$V=r_3+(r_1-r_3)z+(r_2-r_3)w,\\qquad\n W=s_3+(s_1-s_3)z+(s_2-s_3)w.$$ If $P(z,w)=p(V,W)=\\sum_{d,e}P_{de}z^dw^e$ has degree at most $N$, then its normalized degree-$N$ triangular coefficients are $$[p]_{ij}=s_{ij}(P):=\n \\sum_{d=0}^i\\sum_{e=0}^j\n \\frac{\\binom id\\binom je}\n      {\\binom N{d+e}\\binom{d+e}d}\\,P_{de},\n \\qquad i,j\\ge0,\\quad i+j\\le N.$$ On $z,w\\ge0$, $z+w\\le1$, the polynomial is the sum of these coefficients times the nonnegative basis functions $$\\frac{N!}{i!j!(N-i-j)!}\n       z^iw^j(1-z-w)^{N-i-j}.$$ These basis functions sum to one. The same expansion applies entrywise to a symmetric polynomial matrix: positive semidefiniteness of every coefficient matrix implies positive semidefiniteness throughout the triangle.",
        "source_id": "PM-348.3"
      },
      "evidence": [
        {
          "anchor": "stmt-lem-bernstein-conversion",
          "exact_quote": "Let $p$ be a polynomial of degree at most $N$ on an interval $[r,s]$, and write $$p(r+(s-r)z)=\\sum_{j=0}^N a_jz^j.$$ Its degree-$N$ Bernstein expansion is $$p(r+(s-r)z)=\\sum_{i=0}^N h_i\\binom Ni z^i(1-z)^{N-i},\n \\qquad\n h_i=\\sum_{j=0}^i a_j\\frac{\\binom ij}{\\binom Nj}.$$ In particular, $h_i\\ge\\eta$ for all $i$ implies $p\\ge\\eta$ on $[r,s]$.*\n\n*For a polynomial on a triangle with ordered vertices $p_\\ell=(r_\\ell,s_\\ell)$, set $$V=r_3+(r_1-r_3)z+(r_2-r_3)w,\\qquad\n W=s_3+(s_1-s_3)z+(s_2-s_3)w.$$ If $P(z,w)=p(V,W)=\\sum_{d,e}P_{de}z^dw^e$ has degree at most $N$, then its normalized degree-$N$ triangular coefficients are $$[p]_{ij}=s_{ij}(P):=\n \\sum_{d=0}^i\\sum_{e=0}^j\n \\frac{\\binom id\\binom je}\n      {\\binom N{d+e}\\binom{d+e}d}\\,P_{de},\n \\qquad i,j\\ge0,\\quad i+j\\le N.$$ On $z,w\\ge0$, $z+w\\le1$, the polynomial is the sum of these coefficients times the nonnegative basis functions $$\\frac{N!}{i!j!(N-i-j)!}\n       z^iw^j(1-z-w)^{N-i-j}.$$ These basis functions sum to one. The same expansion applies entrywise to a symmetric polynomial matrix: positive semidefiniteness of every coefficient matrix implies positive semidefiniteness throughout the triangle.",
          "source_id": "PM-348.3"
        }
      ],
      "exact_statement": "Let $p$ be a polynomial of degree at most $N$ on an interval $[r,s]$, and write $$p(r+(s-r)z)=\\sum_{j=0}^N a_jz^j.$$ Its degree-$N$ Bernstein expansion is $$p(r+(s-r)z)=\\sum_{i=0}^N h_i\\binom Ni z^i(1-z)^{N-i},\n \\qquad\n h_i=\\sum_{j=0}^i a_j\\frac{\\binom ij}{\\binom Nj}.$$ In particular, $h_i\\ge\\eta$ for all $i$ implies $p\\ge\\eta$ on $[r,s]$.*\n\n*For a polynomial on a triangle with ordered vertices $p_\\ell=(r_\\ell,s_\\ell)$, set $$V=r_3+(r_1-r_3)z+(r_2-r_3)w,\\qquad\n W=s_3+(s_1-s_3)z+(s_2-s_3)w.$$ If $P(z,w)=p(V,W)=\\sum_{d,e}P_{de}z^dw^e$ has degree at most $N$, then its normalized degree-$N$ triangular coefficients are $$[p]_{ij}=s_{ij}(P):=\n \\sum_{d=0}^i\\sum_{e=0}^j\n \\frac{\\binom id\\binom je}\n      {\\binom N{d+e}\\binom{d+e}d}\\,P_{de},\n \\qquad i,j\\ge0,\\quad i+j\\le N.$$ On $z,w\\ge0$, $z+w\\le1$, the polynomial is the sum of these coefficients times the nonnegative basis functions $$\\frac{N!}{i!j!(N-i-j)!}\n       z^iw^j(1-z-w)^{N-i-j}.$$ These basis functions sum to one.       "conclusion": {

        "source_id": "PM-348.3"
      },
      "evidence": [

        }

<a id="pm-s2"></a>
## 2. Definitions, validity and bounds

- **self-dual and anti-self-dual two-form bundles** ([source](#pm-h-4)): ###### The two-block problem.
- **normalized expectation** ([source](#pm-h-4)): For a function $f$, write $\mathbf E f$ for its integral divided by total Riemannian volume.
- **truncated Jacobi determinant** ([source](#pm-h-16)): Put $D_c(t)=D(t)$ before the first conjugate time and $D_c(t)=0$ at and after it.

| id | quantity | relation | value | regime |
|---|---|---|---|---|
| q-diameter-bound | diameter of M | ≤ | $\pi$ | whole manifold |
| q-injectivity-radius | injectivity radius of M | ≥ | $\pi/\sqrt3$ | whole manifold |
| q-weyl-gap | second moment of a nonzero Weyl block norm | ≥ | $1$ | either Weyl block, when not identically zero |
| q-volume-deficit | normalized volume relative to the round value | ≤ | $1-\frac{3}{40}s_0$ | total second moment s_0 |
| q-geodesic-average | unit-tangent-bundle average of the squared curvature deviation | = | $\frac32s_0$ | every time along the flow |
| q-lower-volume | volume in the exceptional moment case | > | $4\pi^2/3$ | exceptional moment value |
| q-sine-lower-constant | rational lower bound constant for pi | = | $\frac{157}{50}$ | constant a_0 |
| q-sine-upper-constant | rational upper bound constant for pi | = | $\frac{22}{7}$ | constant b_0 |
| q-variance-coupling-weight | variance coupling coefficient D | = | $158$ | chosen rational constant |

<a id="pm-s3"></a>
## 3. Proof roadmap

- **step-curvature-blocks** [claim](#stmt-lem-curvature-blocks) / [proof](#proof-stmt-lem-curvature-blocks): Decompose the curvature operator by Hodge type under the Einstein condition and translate sectional positivity into eigenvalue inequalities. -> Weyl blocks with positivity criterion and divergence constraint
- **step-spin-algebra** [claim](#stmt-lem-spin-algebra) / [proof](#proof-stmt-lem-spin-algebra): Highest weight chain argument for commuting rotation algebras. -> weights, tensor product rule and parallel projections
- **step-derivative-equations** [claim](#stmt-prop-weyl-derivative-equations) / [proof](#proof-stmt-prop-weyl-derivative-equations): Casimir projection of the derivative and commutation of covariant derivatives. -> Laplace equations for the block and its derivative
- **step-weak-norm** [claim](#stmt-prop-weak-norm-equation) / [proof](#proof-stmt-prop-weak-norm-equation): Regularize the norm, bound by the refined Kato inequality, and pass to the limit by monotone and dominated convergence. -> distributional norm equation with integrable density
- **step-weyl-gap** [claim](#stmt-prop-weyl-gap) / [proof](#proof-stmt-prop-weyl-gap): Apply the sharp Sobolev inequality to a regularized power of the norm and use Cauchy–Schwarz. -> Weyl-norm gap with equality case
- **step-characteristic** [claim](#stmt-prop-characteristic-identities) / [proof](#proof-stmt-lem-signed-heat-coefficient-2): Heat-supertrace computation on the form complex and Hodge theory. -> Euler and signature formulas in block moments
- **step-upper-volume** [claim](#stmt-prop-upper-volume-deficit) / [proof](#proof-stmt-lem-sine-minorant-2): Polar integration with a discrete Jacobi determinant comparison and a rational sine minorant. -> upper volume deficit
- **step-balanced-moments** [claim](#stmt-prop-balanced-moments) / [proof](#proof-stmt-prop-balanced-moments): Integer parity argument from the characteristic identities and volume deficit, then variance inequality and the moment obstruction. -> equal second moments when both blocks are nonzero
- **step-weighted-hessian** [claim](#stmt-prop-weighted-hessian) / [proof](#proof-stmt-prop-weighted-hessian): Complete the weighted square of the free Hessian component and integrate by parts. -> first-derivative weighted inequality
- **step-certificates** [claim](#stmt-lem-matrix-certificates) / [proof](#proof-stmt-lem-matrix-certificates): Bernstein coefficient expansion on triangular subdivisions with positive semidefinite coefficient matrices. -> finite polynomial signs
- **step-coupling** [claim](#stmt-prop-coupling-estimates) / [proof](#pm-h-30): Group the combined integrand by derivative degree and bound each part by the polynomial certificates. -> quartic, quadratic and potential bounds with nonnegative mean
- **step-one-block** [claim](#stmt-prop-one-block-vanishes) / [proof](#proof-stmt-prop-one-block-vanishes): Complete a square, use strictness of Cauchy–Schwarz on active gradients, force constant norms, and contradict the sectional bound via the two gaps. -> half-conformal flatness
- **step-lower-volume** [claim](#stmt-prop-conditional-lower-volume) / [proof](#proof-stmt-lem-lower-radial-2): Lower polar bound and discrete Jacobi trace expansion with Jensen's inequality, then rational integral bounds. -> strict lower volume bound excluding the exceptional harmonic count
- **step-parallel-models** [claim](#stmt-prop-parallel-models) / [proof](#proof-stmt-prop-parallel-models): Combine the harmonic alternatives, the lower volume bound and the equality case of the gap. -> parallel curvature tensors
- **step-global-isometry** [claim](#stmt-lem-global-isometry) / [proof](#proof-stmt-lem-global-isometry): Continuation of local isometry germs along paths using parallel curvature and simple connectivity. -> global isometry with a model
- **step-orientation-cover** [claim](#stmt-thm-classification) / [proof](#proof-stmt-cor-oriented-classification-2): Pass to the orientation double cover, exclude the complex projective plane by a cohomology argument, and identify the deck map on the sphere as the antipodal map. -> complete classification including real projective four-space

      "inputs": [
        "oriented classification"
      ],

      "output": "complete classification including real projective four-space",

    }
  ]
}

<a id="pm-s4"></a>
## 4. Engineering Triptych

### Panel 1 - epistemic contract

- **inv-bernstein-expansion**: For a polynomial of degree at most N written in the variable z on the interval, the degree-N Bernstein coefficients are h_i = sum over j <= i of a_j times binom(i,j)/binom(N,j), and the Bernstein sum with these coefficients reproduces the polynomial exactly.
- **inv-bernstein-lower-bound**: If every Bernstein coefficient h_i is at least eta, then the polynomial is at least eta on the interval.

### Panel 2 - negative knowledge

- **anti-negative-coefficient-means-negative**: Conclude that the polynomial takes a value below a threshold because some Bernstein coefficient is below it. -> The polynomial can stay above the threshold even when a coefficient is below it, so a rejected certificate says nothing about the sign of the polynomial.
- **anti-degree-exceeds-N**: Apply the degree-N coefficient formula to a polynomial whose degree is larger than N. -> The coefficients computed from the truncated list do not reproduce the polynomial, so any positivity conclusion is unfounded.

### Panel 3 - reference implementation and oracle

Applicability: `constructive`. The Bernstein conversion lemma is a finite exact-arithmetic computation: given N and the rational coefficients a_0..a_N it produces h_0..h_N by an explicit formula. The classification theorem itself is not finite, so only this conversion step is exercised.

```python
# INPUTS: integer N >= 0 and N+1 rational coefficients a_0..a_N of p(r+(s-r)z)=sum a_j z^j, whitespace separated on stdin
# PRECONDITIONS: degree of p is at most N; coefficients are exact integers or fractions p/q
# OUTPUTS: N+1 lines, the Bernstein coefficients h_0..h_N as exact reduced fractions
# TIME: O(N^2) exact rational operations
# SPACE: O(N) rationals
# ARITHMETIC: exact Fraction arithmetic and integer binomials, no floating point
# FAILURE: malformed or short input raises an exception and exits nonzero
import sys
from fractions import Fraction
from math import comb


def main():
    tok = sys.stdin.read().split()
    n = int(tok[0])
    a = [Fraction(t) for t in tok[1:n + 2]]
    for i in range(n + 1):
        s = Fraction(0)
        for j in range(i + 1):
            s += a[j] * Fraction(comb(i, j), comb(n, j))
        print(s)


main()
```

<a id="pm-s6"></a>
## 6. Applicability limits and search

- **limit-closed-einstein-four-manifold** (hypothesis): The classification is stated only for a connected smooth closed Einstein four-manifold with strictly positive sectional curvature. Orientability is not assumed in the theorem, but the main argument works in the oriented setting first and reaches the nonorientable case through the orientation double cover. Blocked use: ['Applying the three-model list to open, incomplete or boundary-bearing manifolds', 'Applying the three-model list to manifolds that are not connected', 'Treating the oriented-case lemmas as valid without passing through the orientation cover for nonorientable manifolds']
- **limit-strict-sectional-positivity** (hypothesis): Strict positivity of sectional curvature is used in an essential way: it makes the Ricci tensor positive definite, fixes the sign of the Einstein constant, and gives the strict inequality on the extremal eigenvalues of the two Weyl blocks. Nonnegative curvature is not covered by this argument. Blocked use: ['Applying the classification to Einstein four-manifolds with nonnegative but not strictly positive sectional curvature', 'Dropping the strict inequality on the extremal eigenvalues in the half-conformal flatness argument']
- **limit-einstein-condition-required** (hypothesis): The block-diagonal curvature splitting uses the Einstein condition to remove the off-diagonal Ricci block. Even then neither Weyl block is forced to vanish, so the vanishing of one block is a deep conclusion and not an immediate consequence of the setting. Blocked use: ['Using the two-block decomposition for positively curved metrics that are not Einstein', 'Assuming a Weyl block vanishes without invoking the coupled estimate']
- **limit-dimension-four-hodge-splitting** (formal_scope): The argument rests on the Hodge star splitting two-forms into two rank-three eigenbundles, which is a four-dimensional structure. The spin-type labels describe representations of the oriented frame group, with no spin structure involved. Blocked use: ['Transferring the Weyl block gap and Hessian estimates to dimensions other than four', 'Assuming a spin structure is available or needed when using the representation labels']
- **limit-exceptional-moment-case** (counterexample_scope): The lower volume bound is conditional on the total second moment taking the exceptional value; it is not a general lower volume estimate. It is used only to exclude the second harmonic alternative. Blocked use: ['Quoting the strict lower volume bound for metrics whose total second moment differs from the exceptional value', 'Reading the strict inequality as a sharp volume lower bound in general']
- **limit-moment-obstruction-existence** (nonconstructive): The moment obstruction is proved by contradiction from a maximizing measure obtained by weak compactness and a separation argument. It gives no explicit extremal measure and no algorithm for finding one. Blocked use: ['Extracting a procedure that computes the extremal measure', 'Using the lemma as an optimisation method for moment-constrained problems']
- **limit-bernstein-sufficient-condition** (edge_case): The Bernstein conversion yields a sufficient condition only: all coefficients bounded below by a threshold imply that bound for the polynomial on the interval. A coefficient below the threshold proves nothing about the polynomial, and the degree must be at most the chosen expansion degree. ### Formal statement (Lean 4)

Corpus status: formalized in the source corpus (toolchain `leanprover/lean4:v4.34.1`, Mathlib `d13f23b723b8a846827a245b89c10fc7d3f11612`). Not re-checked here; the statements give the exact quantifiers and hypotheses, not a proof.

Scope note of family 348 (`lean/docs/348.md`, verbatim):

> The following describes the scope of the Lean formalization related to the following accompanying paper(s):
>
> - [Positively curved Einstein four-manifolds](../../preprints/Positively-curved-Einstein-four-manifolds-September-23-2026/paper.pdf)
>
> ## Scope
>
> The formalization classifies connected smooth closed Einstein four-manifolds with strictly positive sectional curvature. Up to positive scaling and isometry, every such manifold is the round four-sphere, the complex projective plane with its Fubini–Study metric, or round real projective four-space. No orientability hypothesis is required. In the oriented case, only the sphere and complex projective plane occur.

```lean
import Mathlib

namespace OAI

noncomputable section

open scoped Manifold ContDiff BigOperators ENNReal
open Bundle

namespace EinsteinFour

abbrev FourSpace := EuclideanSpace ℝ (Fin 4)
abbrev I4 := 𝓘(ℝ, FourSpace)

def coordinateVector (i : Fin 4) : FourSpace := EuclideanSpace.single i 1

abbrev RoundSphere4 := {x : EuclideanSpace ℝ (Fin 5) // ‖x‖ = 1}

abbrev ComplexProjective2 := Projectivization ℂ (EuclideanSpace ℂ (Fin 3))

abbrev RealProjective4 := Projectivization ℝ (EuclideanSpace ℝ (Fin 5))

def roundDistance (p q : RoundSphere4) : ℝ :=
  Real.arccos (inner ℝ p.val q.val)

def fubiniStudyDistance (p q : ComplexProjective2) : ℝ :=
  Real.arccos (‖inner ℂ p.rep q.rep‖ / (‖p.rep‖ * ‖q.rep‖))

def realProjectiveDistance (p q : RealProjective4) : ℝ :=
  Real.arccos (‖inner ℝ p.rep q.rep‖ / (‖p.rep‖ * ‖q.rep‖))

section Metric

variable (M : Type*) [TopologicalSpace M] [ChartedSpace FourSpace M]
  [IsManifold I4 ∞ M]

abbrev SmoothMetric :=
  ContMDiffRiemannianMetric I4 ∞ FourSpace (TangentSpace I4 : M → Type _)

variable {M}

def metricInChart (g : SmoothMetric M) (c : M) (z : FourSpace) :
    Matrix (Fin 4) (Fin 4) ℝ :=
  let e := chartAt FourSpace c
  let d := mfderiv I4 I4 e.symm z
  fun i j => g.inner (e.symm z) (d (coordinateVector i)) (d (coordinateVector j))

def partialDeriv (f : FourSpace → ℝ) (i : Fin 4) (z : FourSpace) : ℝ :=
  fderiv ℝ f z (coordinateVector i)

def christoffel (g : SmoothMetric M) (c : M) (z : FourSpace)
    (k i j : Fin 4) : ℝ :=
  (1 / 2 : ℝ) * ∑ l : Fin 4, (metricInChart g c z)⁻¹ k l *
    (partialDeriv (fun w => metricInChart g c w j l) i z +
     partialDeriv (fun w => metricInChart g c w i l) j z -
     partialDeriv (fun w => metricInChart g c w i j) l z)

def curvatureComponent (g : SmoothMetric M) (c : M) (z : FourSpace)
    (l i j k : Fin 4) : ℝ :=
  partialDeriv (fun w => christoffel g c w l j k) i z -
  partialDeriv (fun w => christoffel g c w l i k) j z +
  ∑ p : Fin 4,
    (christoffel g c z l i p * christoffel g c z p j k -
     christoffel g c z l j p * christoffel g c z p i k)

def ricciInChart (g : SmoothMetric M) (c : M) (z : FourSpace)
    (i j : Fin 4) : ℝ :=
  ∑ k : Fin 4, curvatureComponent g c z k k i j

def sectionalNumerator (g : SmoothMetric M) (c : M) (z : FourSpace)
    (u v : FourSpace) : ℝ :=
  ∑ l : Fin 4, ∑ m : Fin 4, ∑ i : Fin 4, ∑ j : Fin 4, ∑ k : Fin 4,
    metricInChart g c z l m * curvatureComponent g c z l i j k *
      u i * v j * v k * u m

def IsEinstein (g : SmoothMetric M) : Prop :=
  ∃ lam : ℝ, ∀ (c : M) (i j : Fin 4),
    ricciInChart g c (chartAt FourSpace c c) i j =
      lam * metricInChart g c (chartAt FourSpace c c) i j

def HasPositiveSectionalCurvature (g : SmoothMetric M) : Prop :=
  ∀ (c : M) (u v : FourSpace), LinearIndependent ℝ ![u, v] →
    0 < sectionalNumerator g c (chartAt FourSpace c c) u v

def scaledEDistance (g : SmoothMetric M) (a : ℝ) (x y : M) : ℝ≥0∞ :=
  letI : RiemannianBundle (TangentSpace I4 : M → Type _) := ⟨g.toRiemannianMetric⟩
  ENNReal.ofReal (Real.sqrt a) * Manifold.riemannianEDist I4 x y

def ScaledIsometricTo {N : Type*} (g : SmoothMetric M) (a : ℝ)
    (modelDistance : N → N → ℝ) : Prop :=
  ∃ f : M ≃ N, ∀ x y : M,
    ENNReal.ofReal (modelDistance (f x) (f y)) = scaledEDistance g a x y

end Metric

end EinsteinFour

run_cmd Lean.modifyEnv fun env => Lean.Meta.auxLemmasExt.modifyState env fun _ => {}

namespace PositiveEinsteinFour.Geometry

open EinsteinFour Matrix

variable {M : Type*} [TopologicalSpace M] [ChartedSpace FourSpace M]
  [IsManifold I4 ∞ M]

abbrev Mat4 := Matrix (Fin 4) (Fin 4) ℝ

def chartMetric (g : SmoothMetric M) (c : M) : Mat4 :=
  metricInChart g c (chartAt FourSpace c c)

structure OrthonormalFrame (g : SmoothMetric M) (c : M) where
  matrix : Mat4
  orthonormal : matrixᵀ * chartMetric g c * matrix = 1

def OrthonormalFrame.map {g : SmoothMetric M} {c : M} (e : OrthonormalFrame g c) :
    FourSpace →ₗ[ℝ] FourSpace :=
  (WithLp.linearEquiv 2 ℝ (Fin 4 → ℝ)).symm.toLinearMap ∘ₗ
    (Matrix.mulVecLin e.matrix) ∘ₗ (WithLp.linearEquiv 2 ℝ (Fin 4 → ℝ)).toLinearMap

def OrthonormalFrame.vector {g : SmoothMetric M} {c : M}
    (e : OrthonormalFrame g c) (i : Fin 4) : FourSpace :=
  WithLp.toLp 2 (fun a => e.matrix a i)

structure FrameBundle (g : SmoothMetric M) where
  base : M
  frame : OrthonormalFrame g base

def frameRealization {g : SmoothMetric M} (e : FrameBundle g) :
    M × (Fin 4 → TangentBundle I4 M) :=
  (e.base, fun i => ⟨e.base, e.frame.vector i⟩)

run_cmd Lean.modifyEnv fun env => Lean.Meta.auxLemmasExt.modifyState env fun _ => {}

instance (g : SmoothMetric M) : TopologicalSpace (FrameBundle g) :=
  TopologicalSpace.induced frameRealization inferInstance

/-- An orientation is a clopen choice of determinant sign in every frame fiber. -/
structure FrameOrientation (g : SmoothMetric M) where
  positive : Set (FrameBundle g)
  clopen : IsClopen positive
  nonempty_fiber : ∀ c, ∃ e : OrthonormalFrame g c, (⟨c, e⟩ : FrameBundle g) ∈ positive
  compatible : ∀ c (e f : OrthonormalFrame g c),
    (⟨c, e⟩ : FrameBundle g) ∈ positive →
      ((⟨c, f⟩ : FrameBundle g) ∈ positive ↔ 0 < e.matrix.det * f.matrix.det)

end PositiveEinsteinFour.Geometry

namespace PositiveEinsteinFour

open EinsteinFour

universe u

/-- A connected closed oriented smooth Riemannian four-manifold. -/
structure ClosedOrientedMetricFour where
  manifold : ModelWithCorners.MfldCat.{u} I4 ∞
  [regular : T3Space manifold]
  [secondCountable : SecondCountableTopology manifold]
  [compact : CompactSpace manifold]
  [connected : ConnectedSpace manifold]
  metric : SmoothMetric manifold
  orientation : Geometry.FrameOrientation metric

attribute [instance] ClosedOrientedMetricFour.regular
  ClosedOrientedMetricFour.secondCountable ClosedOrientedMetricFour.compact
  ClosedOrientedMetricFour.connected

section ClassificationDefinitions

variable {M : Type*} [TopologicalSpace M] [ChartedSpace FourSpace M]
  [IsManifold I4 ∞ M]

/-- Positive rescaling identifies the metric with one of the three standard models. -/
def Classified (g : SmoothMetric M) : Prop :=
  ∃ a : ℝ, 0 < a ∧
    (ScaledIsometricTo g a roundDistance ∨
      ScaledIsometricTo g a fubiniStudyDistance ∨
      ScaledIsometricTo g a realProjectiveDistance)

def ClassificationOn (M : Type*) [TopologicalSpace M] [ChartedSpace FourSpace M]
    [IsManifold I4 ∞ M] [T2Space M] [SecondCountableTopology M]
    [CompactSpace M] [ConnectedSpace M] : Prop :=
  ∀ g : SmoothMetric M,
    IsEinstein g → HasPositiveSectionalCurvature g → Classified g

end ClassificationDefinitions

/-- The oriented classification has the round sphere and Fubini--Study models. -/
theorem orientedClassification (X : ClosedOrientedMetricFour.{u})
    (hEinstein : IsEinstein X.metric) (hpositive : HasPositiveSectionalCurvature X.metric) :
    ∃ a : ℝ, 0 < a ∧ (ScaledIsometricTo X.metric a roundDistance ∨
      ScaledIsometricTo X.metric a fubiniStudyDistance) := by
  sorry

section

variable (M : Type u) [TopologicalSpace M] [ChartedSpace FourSpace M]
  [IsManifold I4 ∞ M] [T2Space M] [SecondCountableTopology M]
  [CompactSpace M] [ConnectedSpace M]

theorem classificationOn : ClassificationOn M := by
  sorry

end

/-- The connected closed classification includes the real projective quotient. -/
theorem classification (M : Type*) [TopologicalSpace M] [ChartedSpace FourSpace M]
    [IsManifold I4 ∞ M] [T2Space M] [SecondCountableTopology M]
    [CompactSpace M] [ConnectedSpace M] (g : SmoothMetric M)
    (hEinstein : IsEinstein g) (hpositive : HasPositiveSectionalCurvature g) :
    ∃ a : ℝ, 0 < a ∧
      (ScaledIsometricTo g a roundDistance ∨
        ScaledIsometricTo g a fubiniStudyDistance ∨
        ScaledIsometricTo g a realProjectiveDistance) := by
  sorry

end PositiveEinsteinFour

end

end OAI
```

<a id="pm-s7"></a>
## 7. Complete mathematical paper


We prove the classification conjecture for connected smooth closed Einstein four-manifolds with strictly positive sectional curvature. Up to positive scaling and isometry, every such manifold is the round four-sphere, the complex projective plane with its Fubini–Study metric, or real projective four-space with its round metric. No orientability assumption is needed.

<a id="sec:introduction"></a>
### Introduction

A Riemannian metric is Einstein if its Ricci tensor is a constant multiple of the metric. This fixes the trace of the sectional curvatures while allowing individual curvatures to vary. The classification conjecture in dimension four asks whether the round $S^4$, the round $\mathbb{RP}^4$, and Fubini–Study $\mathbb{CP}^2$ exhaust the connected closed Einstein four-manifolds with strictly positive sectional curvature, up to positive scaling and isometry. Yang explicitly lists these three models in Conjecture 1 of <a id="pm-cite-1"></a>[[Yang2000](#ref-Yang2000)]. We prove the classification in the closed setting.

<a id="stmt-thm-classification"></a>
<div id="thm:classification" class="theorem">

**Theorem 1**. *Let $(M,g)$ be a connected smooth closed Einstein four-manifold with strictly positive sectional curvature, without an orientability assumption. There is a constant $a>0$ such that $(M,ag)$ is isometric to the standard round $S^4$, to $\mathbb{CP}^2$ with its Fubini–Study metric, or to $\mathbb{RP}^4$ with its standard round metric.*

</div>

Strict sectional positivity makes the Ricci tensor positive definite, so the Einstein constant is positive. The oriented classification is the principal task. Until the final subsection of Section <a href="#sec:classification" data-reference-type="ref" data-reference="sec:classification">8</a>, we therefore work on a connected oriented smooth closed four-manifold, choose an orientation, and rescale the metric so that $\operatorname{Ric}=3g$. The final orientation-cover argument uses the oriented conclusion proved there and determines the possible nonorientable quotient.

<a id="pm-h-3"></a>
###### Earlier results.

The problem combines rigidity of the Einstein equation with the global restrictions of positive sectional curvature. Berger’s early rigidity work under strict quarter pinching <a id="pm-cite-2"></a>[[Berger1961](#ref-Berger1961)] was followed by quantitative sectional-curvature bounds of Yang, Costa, and Cao–Tran <a id="pm-cite-3"></a>[[Yang2000](#ref-Yang2000), [Costa2004](#ref-Costa2004), [CaoTran2018](#ref-CaoTran2018)]. These bounds impose additional quantitative hypotheses, some expressed as upper bounds relative to the Einstein constant rather than as pinching ratios. Gursky–LeBrun prove the Fubini–Study conclusion for compact oriented Einstein manifolds with nonnegative sectional curvature and a nonzero positive-definite intersection form <a id="pm-cite-4"></a>[[GurskyLeBrun1999](#ref-GurskyLeBrun1999), Theorem A]. Koca proves the Fubini–Study conclusion for a complete positively sectionally curved Einstein metric Hermitian for an integrable complex structure <a id="pm-cite-5"></a>[[Koca2014](#ref-Koca2014), Theorem 2]. Under the different condition of nonnegative isotropic curvature, the four-dimensional local-symmetry result is due to Micallef–Wang <a id="pm-cite-6"></a>[[MicallefWang1993](#ref-MicallefWang1993), Theorem 4.4]; Brendle extends the Einstein rigidity result to higher dimensions <a id="pm-cite-7"></a>[[Brendle2010](#ref-Brendle2010), Theorem 1].

Recent results impose other geometric or topological conditions. Dameno obtains half-conformal flatness from fibrewise constancy of the scalar curvature of an associated twistor metric, and obtains the standard models in the compact positive-scalar case <a id="pm-cite-8"></a>[[Dameno2026](#ref-Dameno2026), Theorem 1.1 and Corollary 1.2]. Catino–Dameno use separation of the largest and middle eigenvalues of one Weyl block to obtain conformal Kähler geometry after at most a double cover <a id="pm-cite-9"></a>[[CatinoDameno2026](#ref-CatinoDameno2026), Theorems 1.1 and 1.4]. Di Cerbo treats the closed oriented nonflat case with nonnegative sectional curvature and equality everywhere in a Weyl-norm bound, obtaining a locally symmetric $S^2\times S^2$ universal cover <a id="pm-cite-10"></a>[[DiCerbo2026](#ref-DiCerbo2026), Proposition 3.2].

Write $\chi$ for the Euler characteristic and $\tau$ for the signature. Cheng proves the oriented positive-sectional classification under $\chi\le3$, and consequently in the presence of a nontrivial Killing field <a id="pm-cite-11"></a>[[Cheng2026](#ref-Cheng2026), Theorem 1.2 and Corollary 1.4]. His argument combines radial Riccati comparison with Weyl-norm gaps and characteristic-number identities. Gursky–Malchiodi prove the classification for closed oriented Einstein four-manifolds with positive sectional curvature under $2\chi-3|\tau|\le4$, using a Green-function comparison and ADM mass <a id="pm-cite-12"></a>[[GurskyMalchiodi2026](#ref-GurskyMalchiodi2026), Theorem 1.1]. These cited results retain additional curvature, geometric, topological, or symmetry hypotheses. The argument below addresses the interaction of the two Weyl blocks without such an additional restriction.

<a id="pm-h-4"></a>
###### The two-block problem.

The Hodge star on two-forms has eigenvalues $+1$ and $-1$; its two rank-three eigenbundles are denoted by $\Lambda^+$ and $\Lambda^-$. In the normalization $\operatorname{Ric}=3g$, the curvature operator restricts to $\operatorname{Id}+T$ and $\operatorname{Id}+U$ on these bundles, where $T,U$ are symmetric trace-free endomorphisms. They are the two Weyl curvature blocks. The Einstein equation removes the off-diagonal Ricci block but does not force either Weyl block to vanish. Our central intermediate conclusion is *half-conformal flatness*: $$T\equiv0\qquad\text{or}\qquad U\equiv0.$$

Use Hilbert–Schmidt norms and put $$v=|T|/\sqrt6,\qquad b=|U|/\sqrt6,\qquad
 x=-\lambda_{\min}(T),\qquad y=-\lambda_{\min}(U).$$ The exact sectional-positivity condition is $x+y<2$. The inequalities $v\le x\le2v$ and $b\le y\le2b$ imply $v+b<2$, but this weaker norm condition alone is insufficient for the tensor estimate below. Gursky–LeBrun use the corresponding nonstrict cone and norm inequalities and analyze the case of two nonzero Weyl blocks <a id="pm-cite-13"></a>[[GurskyLeBrun1999](#ref-GurskyLeBrun1999), Lemma 1 and Theorem B]. For a function $f$, write $\mathbf E f$ for its integral divided by total Riemannian volume. The gap and its parallel equality case are established in Gursky–LeBrun <a id="pm-cite-14"></a>[[GurskyLeBrun1999](#ref-GurskyLeBrun1999), Theorem 1]: a nonzero block satisfies $\mathbf E v^2\ge1$, or the exchanged inequality for $b$. We derive this statement in our normalization, including zeros of a block.

Scalar coupling of the two blocks also has a clear antecedent. Yang chooses a constant $t$ for which $|W^+|-t|W^-|$ has mean zero and combines the Weyl equation, refined Kato inequality, and Poincaré inequality <a id="pm-cite-15"></a>[[Yang2000](#ref-Yang2000), 6]. Cao–Tran similarly balance regularized powers of the norms <a id="pm-cite-16"></a>[[CaoTran2018](#ref-CaoTran2018), Proposition 2.2]. Here a volume and moment argument forces equality of the two second moments, and a weighted Hessian square supplies the additional tensor estimate.

<a id="pm-h-5"></a>
###### From moment balance to half-conformal flatness.

The characteristic identities relate the two second moments to volume and to the dimensions of harmonic self-dual and anti-self-dual two-form spaces. An upper polar-volume deficit, the two gaps, and a strengthened scalar variance inequality force $\mathbf E v^2=\mathbf E b^2$ when both blocks are nonzero (Proposition <a href="#prop:balanced-moments" data-reference-type="ref" data-reference="prop:balanced-moments">20</a>). This balance controls the mean of $v-b$, enabling the variance inequality to contribute the sign needed in the final integral argument.

The main differential estimate uses the Bianchi identity twice. It first places the gradient of a Weyl block in one irreducible tensor type. The curvature commutator then determines all but one component of its full, generally nonsymmetric Hessian. A curvature-weighted quadratic form is positive on the remaining component because its lower bound is $4-2x-2y>0$. Completing its square with different scalar weights for the two blocks and integrating by parts produces inequalities involving only first derivatives (Proposition <a href="#prop:weighted-hessian" data-reference-type="ref" data-reference="prop:weighted-hessian">23</a>). The proof retains both the derivatives of the scalar weights and the derivative of the curvature operator acting on the matrix slot. This local estimate does not use moment balance.

The two weighted inequalities, a scalar identity, and the variance bound produce an integrand $\mathcal I$ with nonnegative mean. Explicit rational comparison functions give $c\ge1/10$, nonnegative quantities $r,j$ built from the norm gradients, and a scalar $\Theta$ such that $$\mathbf E\mathcal I\ge0,\qquad
 \mathcal I\le-\frac{(\Theta-cr)^2}{c}-c(j-r^2),
 \qquad r^2\le j.$$ The pointwise bounds follow from the weighted Hessian calculation and exact finite polynomial signs. Moreover, $r^2<j$ almost everywhere that either norm gradient is nonzero. Integration therefore makes both norms constant. If both blocks were nonzero, their separate gaps would then contradict $v+b<2$. This proves half-conformal flatness.

The final geometric argument has a different role. Once one block vanishes, the characteristic identities leave two harmonic counts. The exceptional count forces $s_0=\mathbf E(v^2+b^2)=3$ and volume $4\pi^2/3$; a conditional lower polar-volume estimate excludes precisely that case. Equality in the surviving Weyl gap makes the curvature parallel. Matching its tensor with a standard model and using simple connectivity then gives a global isometry. Figure <a href="#fig:proof-map" data-reference-type="ref" data-reference="fig:proof-map">1</a> records the assembly.

<figure id="fig:proof-map">

<figcaption>Assembly of the proof. Moment balance enters the nonnegative integral; the exact pointwise bounds and active-gradient strictness force one-block vanishing. The conditional lower bound identifies the remaining parallel tensor, and simple connectivity permits global continuation.</figcaption>
</figure>

The radial estimates use discrete Jacobi determinants and retain the order of curvature matrices along each geodesic. The lower refinement is conditional on the moment value just described. The article gives the geometric and analytic arguments, together with the finite polynomial formulas and sign proofs. The accompanying checker reproduces the saved finite arithmetic within the precise scope stated in its coverage record.

<a id="pm-h-6"></a>
###### Organization.

Section <a href="#sec:preliminaries" data-reference-type="ref" data-reference="sec:preliminaries">2</a> fixes curvature conventions and derives the differential identities, including their behavior at zeros. Section <a href="#sec:analytic" data-reference-type="ref" data-reference="sec:analytic">3</a> proves the weighted variance and Weyl-gap estimates and states the characteristic identities. Section <a href="#sec:volume" data-reference-type="ref" data-reference="sec:volume">4</a> proves the upper deficit and moment balance. Section <a href="#sec:hessian" data-reference-type="ref" data-reference="sec:hessian">5</a> derives the weighted Hessian inequality, and Section <a href="#sec:coupling" data-reference-type="ref" data-reference="sec:coupling">6</a> combines it with the scalar estimates. Section <a href="#sec:certificates" data-reference-type="ref" data-reference="sec:certificates">7</a> proves the resulting finite signs. Section <a href="#sec:classification" data-reference-type="ref" data-reference="sec:classification">8</a> proves one-block vanishing, the conditional lower volume bound, and the full metric classification. Appendix <a href="#app:analytic" data-reference-type="ref" data-reference="app:analytic">9</a> contains the deferred analytic and heat proofs, while Appendix <a href="#app:topology" data-reference-type="ref" data-reference="app:topology">10</a> proves the required topology.

<a id="sec:preliminaries"></a>
### Curvature blocks and their covariant derivatives

Our first task is to express sectional positivity and the differential Bianchi identity in a common set of variables. The curvature decomposition is classical; see Derdziński . We give the algebra and fix its normalizations, since the later Hessian estimate depends on the precise representation constants. Rescale the metric so that $\operatorname{Ric}=3g$, and write $$d\nu=\frac{d\operatorname{vol}_g}{\operatorname{Vol}(M)},
 \qquad \mathbf E f=\int_M f\,d\nu.$$ The Laplacian $\Delta$ is the trace of the second covariant derivative. Our curvature convention is $$[\nabla_i,\nabla_j]\xi_k=R_{ijka}\xi_a$$ for a covector, with $R_{ijij}>0$ on positively curved orthonormal two-planes. All tensor connections are induced by the Levi–Civita connection. We take covariant derivatives before choosing any pointwise eigenbasis.

<a id="pm-h-8"></a>
#### The two Weyl blocks [sec:coupling](#sec:coupling)

The Hodge-star eigenspaces $\Lambda^+$ and $\Lambda^-$ have eigenvalues $+1$ and $-1$, respectively. On an oriented Euclidean four-space, choose triples of skew orthogonal complex structures $I_1,I_2,I_3$ and $J_1,J_2,J_3$ satisfying $$I_1I_2=I_3=-I_2I_1,\qquad
 J_1J_2=J_3=-J_2J_1,$$ and their cyclic analogues, with every $I_s$ commuting with every $J_t$. The two triples can be constructed from left and right quaternionic multiplication. As two-forms, $I_s/\sqrt2$ and $J_s/\sqrt2$ are orthonormal bases of $\Lambda^+$ and $\Lambda^-$, respectively. Vector and covector indices are identified by the metric.

<a id="stmt-lem-curvature-blocks"></a>
<div id="lem:curvature-blocks" class="lemma">

**Lemma 2** (Curvature blocks and sectional positivity). *There are symmetric trace-free endomorphisms $T\in\operatorname{Sym}_0(\Lambda^+)$ and $U\in\operatorname{Sym}_0(\Lambda^-)$ such that the curvature operator is $$\mathcal R_{\Lambda^2}
 =\begin{pmatrix}\operatorname{Id}+T&0\\
                  0&\operatorname{Id}+U\end{pmatrix}.$$ At a point where these endomorphisms are diagonal, with eigenvalues $t_s,u_s$, the curvature tensor is $$R_{ikjl}
 =\frac12\sum_s(1+t_s)I_{s,ik}I_{s,jl}
  +\frac12\sum_s(1+u_s)J_{s,ik}J_{s,jl}.$$ Put $$x=-\min_s t_s,\qquad y=-\min_s u_s,\qquad
 v=\frac{|T|}{\sqrt6},\qquad b=\frac{|U|}{\sqrt6},$$ where the matrix norms are Hilbert–Schmidt norms. Then $$\sec_g>0\quad\Longleftrightarrow\quad x+y<2,
 \qquad
 v\le x\le2v,\qquad b\le y\le2b.$$ Both Weyl blocks, viewed as curvature four-tensors, have zero divergence. In particular, for $Z=\nabla T$, whose matrix entries are one-forms, the differential Bianchi constraint is $$\sum_s I_s Z_{sr}=0\qquad(r=1,2,3).$$*

</div>

<details id="proof-stmt-lem-curvature-blocks"><summary>Proof of stmt-lem-curvature-blocks</summary>

*Proof.* The first Bianchi identity says that the traces of the two diagonal blocks of the curvature operator agree. Their sum is half the scalar curvature, hence is $6$. Contraction identifies the off-diagonal block with the trace-free Ricci tensor: its nine entries give the coefficients in the independent symmetric trace-free matrices $I_sJ_t$. Thus the Einstein condition makes this block zero, and each diagonal block has trace $3$. Expanding in the orthonormal two-form bases gives the displayed curvature tensor.

A unit simple two-form has self-dual and anti-self-dual parts of norm $1/\sqrt2$. Conversely, a unit two-form whose two parts have these norms has exterior square zero, and is simple: in its orthogonal normal form $\alpha=a\,e^1\wedge e^2+b\,e^3\wedge e^4$, the condition $\alpha\wedge\alpha=0$ is $ab=0$. Therefore all pairs of plus and minus parts of norm $1/\sqrt2$ occur. The smallest sectional curvature is $1-(x+y)/2$, proving the equivalence.

For the norm comparison, write the eigenvalues of $T$ as $-x,a,x-a$. Minimality of $-x$ gives $-x\le a\le2x$, and hence $$\frac32x^2
 \le x^2+a^2+(x-a)^2
 \le6x^2.$$ Division by $6$ gives $v\le x\le2v$. The proof for $U$ is identical.

Contracting the differential Bianchi identity shows that the curvature tensor is divergence-free when Ricci is parallel. Project its last pair of indices onto $\Lambda^+$ or $\Lambda^-$; these projections are parallel. Subtracting the parallel scalar-curvature contribution proves divergence freedom of each Weyl block. In a quaternionic basis, the resulting constraint on $\nabla T$ is exactly the stated identity. ◻

</details>

<a id="pm-h-9"></a>
#### Algebra of the derivative representations

Let $\Sigma_s$ denote the infinitesimal action of $I_s$ on the plus indices of a tensor representation. On $\Lambda^+$ it is rotation about axis $s$ with speed $2$. A complexified irreducible representation has *spin $j$* when $$\sum_{s=1}^3\Sigma_s^2=-4j(j+1)\operatorname{Id}.$$ Pairs $(j,\ell)$ specify the spins of the two commuting rotation algebras. This notation describes associated tensor bundles of the oriented orthonormal frame bundle; it requires no spin structure.

<a id="stmt-lem-spin-algebra"></a>
<div id="lem:spin-algebra" class="lemma">

**Lemma 3** (Representation rules). *A spin-$j$ representation has weights $-j,-j+1,\ldots,j$ for $\Sigma_3/(2i)$, each once, and $$H_j\otimes H_\ell
 \cong H_{j+\ell}\oplus H_{j+\ell-1}\oplus\cdots\oplus H_{|j-\ell|}.$$ In particular, the tangent, plus two-form, and plus trace-free symmetric-matrix representations have types $$\left(\frac12,\frac12\right),\qquad (1,0),\qquad (2,0).$$ The Casimir operators and the orthogonal projections onto these irreducible types are parallel bundle endomorphisms.*

</div>

<details id="proof-stmt-lem-spin-algebra"><summary>Proof of stmt-lem-spin-algebra</summary>

*Proof.* The generators are skew-Hermitian and satisfy $[\Sigma_1,\Sigma_2]=2\Sigma_3$, cyclically. Diagonalize $\Sigma_3/(2i)$ and take the usual conjugate raising and lowering operators, which shift its weight by one. If a unit highest vector has weight $j$, commuting the raising and lowering operators gives the squared lowering coefficient at weight $j-r$ as $$(r+1)(2j-r).$$ Nonnegativity and termination of the chain imply that $2j$ is a nonnegative integer and give the listed weights and Casimir. Taking invariant orthogonal complements decomposes an arbitrary representation. Multiplying the weight lists, and successively removing the chain of highest remaining weight, proves the tensor product rule with multiplicity one. Commuting actions operate on the corresponding multiplicity spaces. The same chain description shows that an intertwiner between single irreducible copies is a scalar.

The actions on the tangent space and on $\Lambda^+$ give the first two types. The symmetric square of the spin-one representation is spin two plus the scalar trace, giving the third. Finally, these splittings are defined by invariant algebraic operators. The connection induced by the orthonormal frame bundle preserves every such operator. Thus the projections are parallel even though a chosen local frame, or a curvature eigenbasis, need not be parallel. ◻

</details>

Write $F=\operatorname{Sym}_0(\Lambda^+)$. For a symmetric trace-free matrix $A$, define the self-adjoint operator on $F$ $$B_F(A)S=\frac{AS+SA}{2}
         -\frac{\operatorname{tr}(AS)}3\operatorname{Id}.$$ It is characterized by $\langle B_F(A)S,S\rangle=\operatorname{tr}(AS^2)$. On any plus spin-$j$ representation with $j\ge1$, define $$Q_j(A)=\frac{\sum_{r,s}A_{rs}\Sigma_r\Sigma_s}{4j(2j-1)}.$$ For diagonal $A$ the numerator is $\sum_s a_s\Sigma_s^2$.

<a id="stmt-lem-quadratic-curvature-action"></a>
<div id="lem:quadratic-curvature-action" class="lemma">

**Lemma 4** (Curvature-weighted operators). *If $A$ has smallest eigenvalue $-x$, then $$Q_j(A)\le \frac{x(j+1)}{2j-1}\operatorname{Id}.$$ Moreover, $Q_2(A)=B_F(A)$. The compression of $B_F(A)$, acting on an $F$ factor, to a highest-spin coupling of total plus spin $j$ is $Q_j(A)$, provided the operator acts trivially on any additional irreducible minus factor.*

</div>

<details id="proof-stmt-lem-quadratic-curvature-action"><summary>Proof of stmt-lem-quadratic-curvature-action</summary>

*Proof.* In a diagonal basis, $a_s+x\ge0$ and every $\Sigma_s^2$ is nonpositive. Thus $$\sum_s a_s\Sigma_s^2
 \le -x\sum_s\Sigma_s^2
 =4xj(j+1)\operatorname{Id},$$ which proves the bound.

To identify $Q_2$, diagonalize $S=\operatorname{diag}(d_1,d_2,d_3)$. The speed-two matrix action gives $|\Sigma_sS|^2=8(d_j-d_k)^2$, with $s,j,k$ cyclic; these three derivatives are mutually orthogonal. Consequently, allowing $A$ to be nondiagonal in this basis, $$\begin{aligned}
 \langle Q_2(A)S,S\rangle
 &=-\frac13\sum_s A_{ss}(d_j-d_k)^2\\
 &=-\frac13\sum_s A_{ss}
       \bigl(2\operatorname{tr}(S^2)-3d_s^2\bigr)
 =\operatorname{tr}(AS^2).
 \end{aligned}$$ Here trace-freeness of both matrices gives the middle identity and removes the scalar term. Polarization proves the operator equality.

By Lemma <a href="#lem:spin-algebra" data-reference-type="ref" data-reference="lem:spin-algebra">3</a>, spin two occurs once in $\operatorname{End}(H_j)\cong H_j\otimes H_j$. Hence an equivariant operator family linear in $A$ is unique up to scalar. The same is true with an irreducible minus factor when the operators commute with its action. Test the scalar at $A=\operatorname{diag}(-1,-1,2)$ on a highest vector. At weight $m$, $$Q_j(A)=\frac{j(j+1)-3m^2}{j(2j-1)},$$ so its highest-weight eigenvalue is $-1$. The $F$ highest vector also has $B_F(A)$ eigenvalue $-1$. A product of highest vectors is the highest vector of the stretched coupling, proving the claimed compression. For the same reason, compression of a single generator from a spin-$\ell$ factor to a stretched spin-$j$ product is $\ell/j$ times the total generator. ◻

</details>

<a id="stmt-prop-weyl-derivative-equations"></a>
<div id="prop:weyl-derivative-equations" class="proposition">

<a id="eq:1"></a>
**Proposition 5** (Differential equations for the Weyl blocks). *The tensor $Z=\nabla T$ belongs to the parallel subbundle $G$ of type $(5/2,1/2)$. On this bundle, put $B_{\mathrm{op}}=Q_{5/2}(T)$. Then $$\Delta T=6(T-N),\qquad
 N=(T^2)_0=T^2-2v^2\operatorname{Id},$$ and $$\Delta Z=(13-20B_{\mathrm{op}})Z.
 \tag{1}\label{eq:1}$$ Every statement has an exchanged counterpart for $U$.*

</div>

<details id="proof-stmt-prop-weyl-derivative-equations"><summary>Proof of stmt-prop-weyl-derivative-equations</summary>

*Proof.* The derivative space $T^*M\otimes F$ has types $(5/2,1/2)$ and $(3/2,1/2)$. The divergence takes values in a space whose plus spins are at most $3/2$. It is nonzero: a nonzero covector tensored with $\operatorname{diag}(1,-1,0)$ has nonzero divergence. It therefore vanishes on the first summand and is injective on the second, by irreducibility. Lemma <a href="#lem:curvature-blocks" data-reference-type="ref" data-reference="lem:curvature-blocks">2</a> puts $Z$ in the first summand.

Let $$C=\sum_s I_s\otimes\Sigma_s$$ on $T^*M\otimes F$. Expansion of the combined Casimir gives $C=-4$ on $G$ and $C=6$ on its complement. In particular $P_G=(6-C)/10$ is a parallel orthogonal projector. The bundle curvature on $F$ is $$F_{ik}=\frac12\sum_s(1+t_s)I_{s,ik}\Sigma_s$$ at a point of diagonalization. Taking the covariant divergence of $CZ=-4Z$, using skewness of $I_s$ to take a commutator and $\sum_{ik}I_{s,ik}I_{r,ik}=4\delta_{sr}$, gives $$-4\Delta T=\sum_s(1+t_s)\Sigma_s^2T
            =-24T+24B_F(T)T=-24(T-N).$$

To differentiate this equation, note first that $\nabla N=2B_F(T)Z$. For each derivative index $j$, the covariant commutation formula reads $$\Delta Z_j
 =\nabla_j\Delta T+\nabla^i(F_{ij}T)
